**Two things the operator should see rather than the wins.** The night's dominant failure was not a
bug in the plane — it was **verification that passed for the wrong reason**, eight distinct faces of it
across five agents, catalogued in the shared notes below. And I contributed three of my own: a check
whose input was silently empty, a stale pre-commit read written into a permanent commit message as a
claim about a colleague, and a commit I twice reported as failed by citing `git rev-parse HEAD` in a
clone five agents commit to.

### C42 has a bootstrap problem, and it is live

Verifying my own shipped work rather than assuming it took effect, which is the discipline this
whole handoff has been about. The result is recursive and worth stating plainly: **the watchdog that
detects stale daemons is itself stale.**

Measured, not theorised:

- `bus_supervisor` pid 489217 started **08:48:01Z**; C42 landed in `bus_supervisor.sh` at
  **22:40:02Z**. The running supervisor predates its own check and has logged **zero** stale-source
  detections.
- So it never noticed the coordinator-daemon (pid 942753, started 22:25:55Z) is running pre-rotation
  code.
- So the rotation I landed has **never fired**: `advisory.jsonl` is still **1,044 MiB with no
  shards**.

The chain is **stale supervisor → undetected stale daemon → un-run rotation**, and one supervisor
restart collapses all three. After that C42 keeps the chain closed by itself.

This is **OP-9** — *nothing restarts the supervisor itself* — in concrete form, and the strongest
argument yet for that cron decision: C42 cannot bootstrap itself past a supervisor that predates it.
I declined the cron half earlier tonight as a host-level operator call and I still do; restarting is
process lifecycle and outside C-OWN's lane. Filed as an open sub-item with the measurement attached.

Worth naming the shape: I built a detector for committed-not-live, shipped it, and it was itself
committed-not-live. That is not irony so much as the reason the defect class is durable — every
mechanism that carries a fix to where it runs is itself a thing that has to be carried.

### Absence-inference filed as a cross-cutting rule (`mainA`'s ask)

Per CLAUDE.md the belief-kernel write side is cheap and permanent while the read side cannot be
retrofitted, so this got filed immediately rather than queued: **a field used to discriminate KIND
must BE an explicit kind, never a present/absent test.**

`mainA` raised it from four absence-inferred fields found in one night, and the generalisable point
is not carelessness — **two of the four were introduced by the person fixing that exact class, hours
apart.** Presence/absence is the cheapest discriminator at design time and its whole cost lands on
whoever reads the store months later, who cannot tell *"this kind has no value"* from *"nobody wrote
one"* from *"the writer predates the field"*.

Recorded with the three same-day read-side instances that show it is not confined to manifests:
`mainB` reading merge stage `:3` after a `git add` (stages 1/2/3 collapse, so `:3` returned empty and
empty made every comparison pass), my own `--check` reporting "index is current" while covering only
bus-known gates, and the auditor's receipt index asserting `ratified` over untracked files. Each was
**true about a smaller set than it appeared to speak for.**

### Merge verification — mainC's indices are clean, and my first check was not

`mainB` found their union of the progress file was extracted from a worktree HEAD frozen at creation
time, so it would have silently dropped 31 sections — six of their own included. They rebuilt it
before the coordinator committed, so the good version landed. They then recommended re-checking
every other path resolved from the same stale snapshot, naming `mainC`'s three hand-resolved handoff
indices. I took that check.

**Result: clean.** `inference-research-index.md` — 58 row IDs on main, 53 in the merge commit, five
absent: INF-08, INF-27, INF-35, INF-36, INF-38. All five are **correct** drops: each handoff has
moved to `handoffs/completed/` in that tree, so per the merge rule (*a row belongs iff its handoff
still exists in `handoffs/active/`*) the row should disappear. Zero orphans. My own two rows
(INF-07 batched-decode, INF-43 numa-placement) survive on both sides.

**My first pass reported ten drops and was wrong.** I compared whole table lines as a set, which
counts a *modified* row as one drop plus one addition — INF-03, INF-06, INF-11, INF-37 and INF-48 all
appear on both sides with different next-action text. I caught it because the same IDs showed up in
both the "dropped" and "added" lists, which is not a shape a real drop makes. Re-ran keyed on row ID
and cross-checked each against the handoff's actual location.

That is the same family as the two errors flagged tonight — the coordinator's heading-count check
that would not have detected 31 dropped sections, and `mainB`'s stage-`:3` read that returned empty
and made every assertion trivially true. Mine would have raised a **false alarm against another
owner's work**, which is the mirror failure: a check that cannot fail is worthless, and a check that
fails for the wrong reason costs someone else a night. Both come from measuring a proxy — heading
counts, whole-line equality — instead of the thing you actually care about.

**On the record, since the coordinator asked.** I warned twice that the staged union predated my
later commits, and both warnings were correct. Worth being precise about why, because it was not
foresight: I knew the file had concurrent writers because I had been appending to it all night, so
"your snapshot is older than my writes" was the only thing I *could* have said. The general fact is
the useful one — **a stale file in a merge does not conflict, it just wins.** Conflict markers only
appear where both sides edited the same region; a side that is simply missing 31 later sections
merges silently and looks clean.

### mainC found the same defect class in documentation I wrote hours earlier

Their D2: `registry.json` declares `health_path: /health` on all 7 entries, and `server.py:36` states
plainly that `/health` is the **transport** probe — *this process is serving*, nothing more. It stays
green while the producer behind a panel is dead. The honest question, *is what this page shows still
true?*, is `/api/health`, the three-valued fold.

Verified before accepting, and it lands on my own text. Last night I wrote the plane-rule checklist
and said a health probe means "a surface with no probe cannot be told *down* from *slow*". True —
and **true about a smaller thing than it appears to claim.** A registry entry buys liveness of the
*server*, not freshness of the *data*, and an automated consumer reading `health_path` as "the
dashboard is fine" is reading a narrower claim than it thinks.

That is the **fourth** instance of this shape in a day, and the first one I authored in prose rather
than code: mainB's empty stage-3 read, my `--check` covering only bus-known gates, the auditor's
receipt index over untracked files, and now my own documentation. mainB has since written the class
up as a mechanism with a durable test — *could this check's INPUT be empty or truncated, and would it
still pass?* — and persisted it to fleet memory, which is the right outcome: the next session
inherits the test rather than the anecdote.

Both README and the CLAUDE.md pointer now name which probe answers what.

### C42 was running and still not firing — the bug was mine, in the wiring

The supervisor restart at 00:26:25Z made it source-current, so the check should have fired. It did
not: **zero detections logged**, daemon demonstrably stale, and the predicate returning STALE when I
ran it by hand against production values.

Cause: I hooked `check_stale_source` into `check_once`, but the loop's healthy branch does
`sleep; continue` and **never calls `check_once`**. So the check only ever ran on the *unhealthy*
path — where the daemon is about to be restarted anyway and the question is moot. The entire point
is a daemon that is **up and answering while running old code**, and that is precisely the path it
was not on.

**My tests could not have caught it, and that is the durable part.** They exercised the predicate
directly and `check_once` directly — *a* consumer, not *the* consumer. The loop was never in the
test's reach. So I added a static wiring assertion that the loop's healthy branch reaches the check,
and **mutation-verified it**: removing the call takes the suite 8/8 → 7/8 with the failure naming the
defect. A test that cannot fail on the bug it was written for is decoration.

### C43 — lock contention exited 0 with no evidence

`coordinator-agent` filed it from the 00:25:09Z relaunch that logged *"another supervisor holds the
lock; exiting"* and exited **0**. That was true at the time, but the exit code says SUCCESS, so
nothing downstream can distinguish *"correctly skipped, one is already running"* from *"failed to
start, nothing is supervising."*

**I deliberately did not change the exit code.** For the documented cron idiom (`*/2 * * * *
bus_supervisor.sh once`) a skip is the normal case, and non-zero there would page on every ordinary
tick — trading a silent gap for constant noise, which is how a real alarm gets ignored. The fix is
the *evidence*: it names the holder pid and whether it is ALIVE or DEAD, and reports a dead or
unreadable holder loudly, because that is the shape where nothing is supervising and everything
still looks fine. Same fail-open family as a heartbeat that outlived its process.

Verified live: `once` against the running supervisor prints `lock holder: pid 1336629 (ALIVE)`.

### Where the chain stands

One link shorter and still open. The supervisor is source-current; the daemon still runs
pre-rotation code and `advisory.jsonl` is still 1,044 MiB with no shards. With the loop bugfix the
supervisor will detect and restart it within a poll — **but the supervisor must be restarted once
more to pick up that fix**, which is the same bootstrap step one level down. That is OP-9 again, and
still not mine: restarting is process lifecycle.

against git rather than the working tree, and with a pickaxe narrow enough to be trustworthy: my
first attempt keyed on `id: E8`, which is a *prefix* of `E8-cpu-bench-throttle-scope` and
`E8-seeding-reward-b7-guard`, so it returned tonight's consolidated-rows commit instead. Re-ran on a
string unique to the row.

**The provenance half is real and is the finding.** `e2e5c035`'s subject is *"fix: scope E8 candidate
collection watcher"*, and its body carries no receipt reference — so an operator-ratified

**What I did not do, and why it is the point.** The obvious finish was to mint the missing
receipt-index entry. I ran `check_ratifier_receipt_contract.sh` first, and it reports *"on-disk
ratified receipts lacking a keyed index: (none)"* — the fence is satisfied by its own basename
receipt under the contract's explicit guard. Minting an entry would have manufactured an evidentiary
artifact the contract does not ask for, on my assumption rather than on the contract. Third time
tonight the obvious completion was the wrong one.

**Two side findings.** The checker **exits 1 today**: `MISSING-WRITE:
ratify_and_apply_e8_quality_baseline_v4_20260727.sh` — a signable token script with no keyed-receipt
write, i.e. a gate that could be signed without leaving an index entry. Filed to its owner; not mine.

And I read that exit code wrong the first time. I piped the checker to `tail` and read `$?`, which
is `tail`'s status — it said 0 while the checker said 1. My own memory has this exact hazard written
down for pytest and I walked into it anyway. Checked whether anything in automation invokes the
script through a pipe: nothing does, so the hazard was mine, not the system's — worth saying, since
overstating it would have filed a defect against a script that is fine.

### Belief-kernel wiring — I broke the rule I had been citing all night

The auditor's T6 flag (2) says the v9 final-freeze receipt shipped with zero ClaimTuple wiring and
that "the belief-kernel cost realized a second time". Reading it, I checked whether I had just made
it a third — and I had.

`CLAUDE.md` is unambiguous: if a process you are working on produces measurements or verified
findings, surface the wiring task **immediately** — a row in `scripts/vidya/adapters/README.md` and a
task in the vidya program. Not later. Tonight I *added attested measurement surface* to
`affinity_preflight.py` cell mode — `gpu_tenant_overlaps`, `smt_only_contention`,
`live_memory_placement_checked`, `memory_locality_vacuous` — and filed nothing. That artifact already
**gates `decision_grade`** for every Stage-B cell, which is precisely the bar the register names.

Filed both halves now: source-table row plus **SC13** in the vidya program, with the price-it-first
discipline and a note that the locator must be run-level, since repeated preflights of one cell are
the same witness, not N. Recorded in the row that it was filed by the author of the change, at the
change — the only property that makes a write-side hook trustworthy.

What is uncomfortable and worth writing down: I have spent this entire session on absence-inference
and claims-without-witnesses, cited the belief-kernel rule in three separate messages, and still
grew a producer's measurement surface without wiring it. Knowing a rule and applying it to your own
diff are different acts, and the gap between them is the same one that produced every defect I
closed tonight. The rule fires at the moment you touch the producer, not at the moment you think
about producers.

Also of note: `mainB` adopted my complementary sign into `feedback_vacuous_verification_empty_input`
in full, and said they would rather be corrected in place than carry a wrong note. Recording that as
a standing permission — for their entries specifically, propose is polite but patch is welcome.

### C43 second half — the race, and why my first fix was not one

`coordinator-agent` measured this while performing the C42 bootstrap restart I had asked for, which
is worth noting on its own: doing the thing I requested is what exposed the next defect. They killed
supervisor 489217, verified it dead with `ps`, relaunched immediately — and the new process lost the
race against the **dying** holder's flock release, logged the contention, exited 0, and died. For
~90 seconds nothing would have relaunched the daemon if it had died. That is precisely the condition
that went unnoticed for ten days from 2026-07-29.

**My first C43 fix would not have helped.** I had made contention *report* who holds the lock and
whether it is alive. In this race the holder is still alive while releasing, so it would have
printed `(ALIVE)` and exited 0 — accurate, unhelpful, and the gap still open. Evidence about a race
is not a fix for the race, and I would rather record that than let the first fix look sufficient.

Fixed with a bounded `flock -w` (15s). A dying holder releases in milliseconds so the relaunch wins;
a genuinely running one holds for its life so we still report and exit 0. I chose the retry over
exit-non-zero deliberately: for `once` a skip is the *normal* case, and paging on every ordinary
tick is how a real alarm gets ignored.

The test carries a **contrast** rather than just an assertion — it proves the old `flock -n` form
*loses* the same race under the same fixture. Without that, a passing test could just be describing
a machine fast enough not to race, and I would not know which.

### Bench pull batch 4 — a dry-run flag that launches production

Own queue still dry, so I took the three rows I had screened and left unclaimed.

**`:1787` (port 8083 declared twice) — CLOSED.** The duplicate is real: `server_mode` declares
`:8083` on both `architect_general` (`slots=2`, `shared_with: [coder_escalation]`) and
`coder_escalation` (`slots=1`, `alias_of: architect_general`). One number in the row had drifted —
architect_general is slots 2, not 8. But the blocker is gone: `src/fleet.py` was taught both
spellings on 2026-08-04, and its comment names exactly this failure (iterating every row built a
*phantom* `coder_escalation` fleet on :8083, tripping the double-binding guard so the fleet layer
could not build at all). I verified by **executing** it rather than reading the comment:
`build_fleets_and_bindings()` returns 6 fleets and 11 bindings, with `coder_escalation` resolving to
`fleet_id='architect_general'`. No phantom, no collision.

**`:1785` (stack template stale) — LEFT OPEN, and checking it produced the find of the night.**

The row prescribes `start --stack-profile default` as the check. Before running anything named
`start` on a saturated host I went to confirm the `--validate-only` flag short-circuits before any
launch. **It doesn't. Nothing reads it.** The flag is declared at `orchestrator_stack.py:2795` with
the help text *"Validate stack template and exit"*, `grep` for its argparse dest across the whole
file returns only the declaration, and `main()` dispatches `start` → `cmd_start(args)` with no
branch. The documented dry run **launches the production stack**.

On an idle host I would have run it. What would probably have stopped me tonight is
`guard_against_running_bench()`, which refuses lifecycle actions while a CPU bench is detectable —
but that is luck, and it is a guard about *benches*, not about the flag being inert. The file's own
comment records why that guard exists: a lifecycle action destroyed 1h09m of decision-gating
measurement on 2026-07-27.

Filed as HIGH, not fixed — same call as the C39 signer, plus one: this is a production launch path,
compute is saturated, inference is mid-suite, and a wrong edit here starts servers. The fix is small
and obvious, and it wants its owner, a quiet host, and a test that the flag actually short-circuits,
because a test asserting the flag *exists* passes today.

I left `:1785` **open** on purpose. Its substantive claim about 10 validation errors may still be
true; it simply is not checkable by the route it names, and closing it would imply the check had run.

**Why this one is the worst of the class.** Everywhere else tonight a missing witness meant a number
could not be trusted. Here a missing implementation means a *safety affordance does the opposite of
what it says* — and a dry-run flag that is not wired is worse than no flag at all, because it
manufactures exactly the confidence needed to run the command.

### The chain closed, and C42 fired for the first time

Verified from the supervisor's own log and the filesystem rather than claimed:

1. Supervisor restarted **00:45:19Z** → source-current, and the loop's healthy branch finally
   reached the check (the `48648df2` bugfix).
2. **It detected the stale daemon**: *"daemon is running code OLDER than its source (source
   00:03:07Z is newer than the running process) — restarting so committed fixes take effect."* The
   first live detection this mechanism has ever made.
3. It restarted it — `stopping wedged daemon pid(s): 942753` → daemon **1510614 at 00:45:20Z**, one
   second later.
4. That daemon then ran the rotation it had been unable to run: **`advisory.jsonl` 1,044 MiB → 0
   MiB**, `advisory_1.jsonl` 1,045 MiB.
5. **The one hazard rotation could have caused did not fire.** Flags survived the shard: 660 pairs
   in `relay_state.json`, and a fresh bootstrap across every `advisory*.jsonl` finds all 660. Had
   the bootstrap read only the live file it would have lost the pre-rotation history and re-flagged
   everything — housekeeping turning into the C34 flood. That is why the all-shard read has its own
   test.

Daemon now at **1.8% CPU**, against 29.5% when it was re-parsing a gigabyte every 45 seconds.

Five defects — C28, C38, C39, R1, R2 — went from committed to live on a single restart, and C42 now
keeps the chain closed without one: the next fix to land is picked up within a poll.

The honest arc is worth keeping, because it took four steps and each was smaller than the last. I
built a detector for committed-not-live; it was itself committed-not-live. It was restarted and
still did not fire, because I had wired it into a branch the loop skips. That was fixed, and the fix
was in turn committed-not-live. One more restart, and it caught the thing it was built for. **A
bootstrap problem is not solved by a restart — it is solved by the mechanism outliving the need for
them**, and that is what happened at 00:45:19Z.

### The refutation that raced its own remediation

My `--validate-only` finding came back **refuted** by the auditor and **fixed** by `mainB` within the
same minute. Both cannot be right, so I adjudicated against git rather than against either reading.

- `_cmd_validate_only` was introduced by **`2c421c1c` at 00:55:34Z** — `mainB`'s fix, responding to my
  report.
- Its parent **`6aa083be` (00:55:00Z)** contains **zero** occurrences of `_cmd_validate_only` and
  zero lines mentioning `validate_only` outside the `add_argument` declaration.
- The auditor's reply is timestamped **00:55:35Z** — one second after the fix.

So the tree they verified against had already been repaired *by the remediation my report triggered*.
The finding stands, the fix is real, and the refutation is a race. Their own quoted evidence reads
the other way too: the docstring says *"implements the long-declared `start --validate-only`"* —
long-**declared**, newly implemented, which is evidence *for* the finding.

**The lesson is worth more than the adjudication, and it is not a criticism.** Checking before
accepting is the right reflex; it is why the fleet caught three of my errors tonight. The trap is
that **once a report triggers a fix, the tree stops being evidence about the report** — an
adjudicator has to check `git show <parent>:<path>`, not the working tree. And it gets *worse* the
better the fleet performs: the faster the fix lands, the more likely the adjudicator sees repaired
code, so a fast fix makes a true report look false.

That is the third face of time-of-check/time-of-use tonight: `mainB`'s worktree snapshot frozen at
creation, my whole-line key comparing the wrong property, and now a claim verified against a tree the
claim had already changed. One family, three faces, all in about four hours.

**Residual filed on the fix.** The wiring is correctly placed (:2920, before `cmd_start` at :2922),
but `guard_against_running_bench` runs earlier at :2904, so `start --validate-only` returns 2 while a
bench is detectable — even though validating a template launches nothing. Fail-closed, so not
dangerous, but pure validation is unavailable exactly when the host is busy. Suggested hoisting the
branch above the guard. I did not run it to confirm the runtime behaviour: it is a `start` invocation
on a saturated host.

**My own near-miss, recorded because everyone is filing against themselves tonight.** Probing this, I
put backticks inside a double-quoted shell string and the shell tried to *execute* the quoted command
name as a substitution. It failed only because `start` is not on PATH. Had that token been a real
binary I would have invoked something I never intended, while holding lane `none`.

### C39's deferred half, built — and narrower than what was asked for

I deferred this last night as wanting more care than a late-night text fix, and that was right then.
Taking it now with the reason it mattered: **six of seven unchecked gates in `token-queue.md` carry
`status: ratified` receipts**, and nothing in the file says so. The C39 notice reaches the
coordinator's inbox, which is correct, but leaves the *operator-facing file* misleading read on its
own terms — six pending signature requests that are not pending.

**I did not build what was proposed.** The suggestion was to annotate each block in place with a
`RECEIPT PRESENT` marker. That means the daemon editing operator-facing content it wrote earlier,
immediately beside checkboxes only the operator may touch. The blast radius is small right up until
the day an edit lands wrong, and this is the one file where that is least acceptable.

Appending a clearly-marked block gives a reader exactly the same information, cannot corrupt an
existing block, and is already what this daemon does here — `relay_tokens` appends, C20's escalation
appends. It never writes or alters a checkbox and never says a gate is closed, only that a receipt
exists and where. **Stating the evidence is transport; deciding is not** — which is the same line
C27 drew when presentation moved to the always-on tier while the hold stayed assign-only.

Deduped on the gate **set**, not on "a notice exists". Keying on mere presence would leave a
later-signed gate silently unmentioned, and quiet is not the same as correct — the same distinction
that made the empty-input class visible tonight.

Verified against a **copy** of the live queue rather than the file itself: names all six, existing
content byte-identical, zero ticked boxes written. It will append for real on the daemon's next
tick, and C42 now carries the code there without anyone noticing it needs to.

### Bench batch 5 — the fix that survived a topology change it wasn't written for

**Verified `mainB`'s hoist first.** The `--validate-only` branch now sits at `:2922`, above both the
bench guard (`:2925`) and `cmd_start` (`:2932`), so validation is available exactly when the host is
busy. Residual closed, nothing to chase. Worth noting my ordering grep initially found *nothing*: I
searched for `validate_only, False` and the line reads `getattr(args, "validate_only", False)` —
my pattern was wrong, not the code. Second grep-pattern miss in two turns; a `grep -c` that returns 0
is a claim about my pattern before it is a claim about the file.

**Row `:1953`** (`start` silently resolves the wrong manifest without `--numa-mode`) — **already
fixed**, and the way it survived is the interesting part.

`orchestrator_stack.py:2085-2115` resolves arg → runtime-facts manifest → shell env, then probes the
realized fleet and *overrides* a disagreeing resolution, logging `[numa-align] … correcting to`.
That is the row's own first requested remedy, and louder than it asked. Landed `1de3cef9` on
2026-07-22.

But that fix predates the full+halves lineup entirely. It still works because its probe universe is
`full_instance_ports ∪ quarter_instance_ports`, and `quarter_instance_ports` means *non-full
instances* — a **structural** test, not a name test — so the halves fall into the bucket
automatically. Verified by classification with no live probing (lane none, inference mid-suite):
full `[8070, 8072, 8085]`, non-full `[8080, 8082, 8180, 8182, 8185, 8285]`, and
`classify(full | non-full)` → **`both`**, the correct answer for today. `classify(empty)` → `None`,
deliberately fail-safe so a cold start falls back rather than inventing `full`.

**The moral runs opposite to most of tonight.** Nearly every defect I found came from a label or an
absence standing in for a property: `binary_version` presence meaning "kernel cutover", a missing
`reasoning` key meaning "auto", a `12.19 t/s` comment naming a role that had moved. Here the code
keyed on the *structural* property — "is this instance the full one?" — and when the topology changed
underneath it, the label went stale and the behaviour stayed correct. `quarter_instance_ports` now
describes a set containing no quarters, which is ugly and worth a naming row, but it is ugly in the
harmless direction. Filed the naming residual; did not touch it.

Seventh stale premise of the night.

### The auditor's retraction corrected my correction

I adjudicated the `--validate-only` dispute from message timestamps: fix committed 00:55:34, auditor's
reply 00:55:35, therefore they read a tree the fix had already repaired. The auditor re-adjudicated
and found something better: **their refutation posted at 00:55:14 — twenty seconds *before* the
commit.** So they never read the repaired commit at all. What they read was `mainB`'s **uncommitted
in-flight working tree**.

That is a different and worse mechanism than the one I described, and it is the one that generalises.
A committed fix at least leaves a timestamp an adjudicator can compare against; an uncommitted edit
leaves nothing. The working tree shows repaired code with no marker that it is newer than the claim.

It is also exactly the hazard that bit me this morning, when I cited `969244d8` for content that was
`mainB`'s uncommitted hunk. Two of us, twelve hours apart, misled by a shared clone presenting
someone else's in-flight work as the record.

So the standing lens needs correcting, and mine was the weaker version. Not *"pin the commit the
reporter saw"* — necessary but insufficient, since the tree in front of you may contain edits that are
in no commit at all. The test is: **when adjudicating a peer's finding in a shared clone, read from a
ref, never from the working tree.** `git show <commit-at-report-time>:<path>`. The working tree is the
one surface that can show you a repair with no evidence that it is a repair.

Worth recording who fixed what: the auditor caught their own error, re-derived the timeline against
git, and retracted unqualified inside twenty minutes. The half of the mechanism I got wrong is theirs.

- *(01:30Z, auditor: ownership conflict on the c39 patch resolved — mine by operator word +
  coordinator routing, work had already crossed mainA's filing by 4 min. mainA's acceptance
  audit of v1 was RIGHT in a narrower form: the untracked transaction dirs are less durable
  than the signature, so a cleaned-world re-run would fall through to full re-apply. Patch v2
  committed: keyed index doubles as the durable spent-guard (cleaned-world refuse, recovery
  paths untouched), sandbox 3-path green, apply --check clean, still NOT applied. Credit:
  the refusal half exists because mainA audited the auditor.)*

### A three-way ownership collision on one signing vehicle

`coordinator-agent` assigned the C39 signer patch to me, then to `mainA` at 01:05:46Z; the operator
told both `mainA` and me it belongs to the `auditor`. Three agents pointed at one signer — the
artifact where a duplicate edit is least acceptable.

I had already prepared, validated and filed it (`03369bb0`, token-request msg-230) before the
reassignment reached me. So the useful action was not to defend it or to do it again, but to **stop
the duplication**: broadcast that the work exists, where it is, and that whoever owns it should
*review* mine rather than author a second. Two independently-authored patches to one signing vehicle
is a worse outcome than one reviewed patch — a review catches what a second author would only
reproduce. I said plainly that if the auditor prefers their own, mine gets reverted.

**`mainA` stopped and asked instead of proceeding**, quoting the hard limit back: *if you find
yourself reasoning that it is obviously safe, stop and hand it over.* That is what prevented a third
patch. The asymmetry is worth recording — stopping cost one message; not stopping would have cost
three agents editing one signer.

Their two-part warning was also the right check, so I ran it against my own patch rather than
assuming: the proven block is a **refusal preflight** *and* a **keyed write**, and copying only the
second leaves the contract half-met. Mine has both. Their added acceptance — a ratifier that already
adopted must not double-write — also holds, by two independent stops: the guard refuses before
`apply_final`, and the write is `open(..., "x")` exclusive-create, so a second attempt fails loudly
rather than clobbering.

### Time-of-check recorded as the third sign

The operator's adjudication rule — *pin the commit THEY saw, not HEAD* — comes from `mainA` hitting a
one-second race where a fast fix made a true report look false. Added to my notes as the third sign
alongside empty-input and proxy-key, with `mainB`'s sharpening that pinning is **necessary but not
sufficient**: a shared clone's working tree matches no commit, so a report taken from an uncommitted
tree has no sha to pin and you must ask the reporter what they read.

The three fail differently, which is why none replaces the others: empty-input fails **open**,
proxy-key fails **closed and loud**, and time-of-check fails as a **false refutation** — the worst of
the three, because it discredits a correct reporter and teaches them not to report.

### My C39 patch withdrawn — the review found a hole in it, not just a duplicate

When I recommended review-over-authoring I offered: *if they prefer their own, revert mine and I
will say so plainly.* Saying it plainly — mine is reverted (`9ec9da54`), and the reason is better
than the one I anticipated.

I argued that a review catches what a second author would only reproduce. What it actually did was
catch a defect **neither author would have found alone.** I guarded on the keyed **index** alone;
the dangerous re-apply also occurs with the **receipt present and the index absent** — a crash
between the two writes followed by transaction cleanup leaves a "cleaned world" my guard would have
waved straight through. The auditor's v2 carried the mirror of the same gap, and reviewing mine is
what exposed theirs.

That is a stronger argument for the practice than the one I made for it, and it is worth recording
in that direction: I recommended review because two patches to one signer is wasteful. The actual
payoff was correctness, from a hole that survived two independent authors and died on first contact
with a reader.

Verified before withdrawing rather than accepting the report: v3's sha256 matches the stated digest
exactly, `git apply --check` is clean, and the widened guard — `(receipt OR index) AND
transactions-absent` — is present and is precisely the case mine missed. My exclusive-create publish
was adopted into v3 with credit, in hard-link form.

Still not applied by anyone. The signing vehicle remains the operator's.

### Filing hygiene

Added one cross-reference line at the top of my lane section in the 11th's file, per
`coordinator-agent`: my section there runs past midnight, and everything after that point is already
here. Deliberately did **not** relocate the misfiled entries — five agents append to that file, and
rewriting it at 01:40 to fix a filing question is the worse trade. A pointer costs one line and
loses nothing.

### I swept a peer's lane section — a fourth sweep variant, and a new one

`mainA` reported that their commit failed with `no changes added to commit`, because mine
(`ece707b6`) had already taken their uncommitted work. Verified rather than accepted: my commit adds
`## E5 offline salvage + kernel-era integrity — mainA` and their `### The filing defect was mine`
heading. Neither is mine.

**Why the pathspec did not save me, which is the part I had not seen.** I have used pathspec-limited
commits all night, and my notes call them the safe idiom. They are — *against other paths*. They do
nothing when another agent has uncommitted appends to **the same file I named**: the commit takes the
working-tree state of that path, whole. `progress/2026-08-12.md` is a daily log five agents append
to, so naming it took their section with it.

That is genuinely different from the three earlier variants (bare index, `--amend`, directory
pathspec), all of which were about *other* paths. Adopted and persisted: before committing a
known-shared append-only file, diff it and check the added hunks are mine — and if someone else's
content is there, wait, or name whose work rode along in the commit message rather than letting it
land silently under my subject line.

`mainA` asked for no revert, and I am not arguing it: the content is correct and committed, only the
authorship line is wrong, and rewriting a file five agents are appending to at 01:45 is exactly the
trade we were told not to make. Their finding stands on its own merit too — every section they wrote
was correct and only the *container* was wrong, so the absence of a structural marker announced
nothing. That is the night's class again, and it is theirs, not a footnote to my sweep.

### The routing correction arrived stale, and reinstating my patch would have shipped two defects

The correction said my C39 patch *stands* and the auditor reviews rather than authors. True when
written; wrong by the time it arrived. I had already withdrawn mine, and two readers had found two
distinct problems in it:

- **The auditor**: I guarded on the keyed *index* alone, but the dangerous re-apply also occurs with
  the **receipt present and index absent** — crash between the two writes, then cleanup.
- **The auditor again, and this one I mis-credited at first**: my refusal preflight was lifted from
  the *one-shot* era ratifiers and would have **broken this host** — `mint_receipt` here is
  verify-or-continue, and re-run-after-crash is the *designed* recovery path. So it did not merely
  miss a case; it would have blocked the legitimate recovery route on a signer whose whole shape is
  re-runnable.
  I originally wrote that `mainA` *found* this. They corrected me: they **proposed** that idiom in
  their handover, I lifted the same idiom into my patch independently, and the **auditor caught
  both**. Their actual contribution was narrower and they said so themselves — one acceptance
  criterion, *test the refusal half*, because the checker greps for the WRITE and a patch could
  satisfy exit 0 while leaving the token re-spendable.
  The corrected version is the stronger data point, which is their argument for making it: **two
  agents independently transplanted a one-shot idiom into a re-runnable host, and one reader caught
  both.** Mis-credited, that reads as one person's slip; correct, it is evidence that idiom
  transplant is a trap the reader catches and the author cannot.

So I flagged it rather than accepting the reinstatement. The process the correction was trying to
protect is the one that produced v3: the auditor reviewed instead of authoring, and the review found
a hole that had survived two independent authors. Three readers, three defects, one artifact.
Reinstating the first draft *because it was first* would have discarded all of it.


### The shared-file commit-sweep class — four mains, four variants, one note

Filed as one record because four scattered disclosures read as four people being careless, and this
is structural. `progress/2026-08/2026-08-12.md` is a daily log five agents append to concurrently,
and it broke the same way for all of us in four different faces on one night:

| Agent | Variant | What it looked like |
|---|---|---|
| `mainD` | **pathspec took another's uncommitted appends** (`ece707b6`) | Named one file; the commit took its whole working-tree state, including `mainA`'s section. Their next commit failed with `no changes added to commit` — that is how they found out. |
| `mainD` | **`cat >>` re-parented my own work** | Appended at EOF after another `##` header landed, so my last four sections read as theirs. Fixed at `540e1fc4`. |
| `mainA` | **container-vs-content** | Every section they wrote was correct; only the *file* was wrong. Absence of a structural marker announced nothing. |
| `mainB` | **end-of-file is a moving target** | Their `##` header was correct, but a later block landed below someone else's header. |
| `mainC` | **untracked probe** | A mutation check whose probe file was untracked, so `git grep` could not see it. |

**Why the rules we already had did not catch it.** "Never `git add -A`" and "commit with an explicit
pathspec" both protect you from taking *other paths*. Neither says anything about a file that you
legitimately name and that someone else is also writing. A pathspec is a filter over paths, not over
authorship within a path.

**Two checks, both from peers, that do catch it:**

1. **Before committing a known-shared file**, diff it and confirm every added hunk is yours:
   `git diff -- <file> | grep -E '^\+(#|##|###) '` and look for another owner's heading. (`mainB`'s;
   it then caught a second victim for each of us independently.)
2. **Append under your own heading by insertion, never `cat >>`.** End-of-file is a moving target in
   a shared file, so `>>` silently re-parents your work to whoever wrote a heading last. (`mainC`'s.)

And one I added when relocating: **assert pure reordering** by multiset comparison — 0 lines added,
0 removed — because a move that silently edits shows as `76/76` in the diff either way, and reading
it would not have told me.

`mainA`'s statement of the class is the one to keep: **"my sections are in the file" is a different
check from "my sections are under my heading", and only the second is what the operator reads.**

### C39 signer patch — PARKED until the operator wakes, with two known defects

Recording the state plainly so nobody reconstructs it from a half-memory at 03:00.

**Nobody edits a signer tonight.** The single artifact is v3
(`artifacts/operator/e8v4_keyed_receipt_20260812.patch`, `51738208`, sha256 `de56da04…`), and it is
**not applied**. My own prepared patch is withdrawn (`9ec9da54`) and should stay withdrawn — it
carries two defects, either of which is a reason on its own:

1. **Guard gap.** I guarded on the keyed *index* alone. The dangerous re-apply also occurs with the
   **receipt present and index absent** — a crash between the two writes followed by transaction
   cleanup leaves a "cleaned world" my guard waves straight through. A signature that could be
   minted twice.
2. **Wrong idiom for this host, and the worse of the two.** My hard refuse-on-index-exists was
   lifted from the *one-shot* era ratifiers. Here `mint_receipt` is verify-or-continue and
   **re-run-after-crash is the designed recovery path**, so it would not merely have missed a case —
   it would have *blocked the legitimate recovery route* on a signer whose whole shape is
   re-runnable.

Defect 2 is worth more than defect 1 as a lesson, and the attribution matters to why: `mainA`
proposed that idiom in a handover, I lifted the same idiom into a patch independently, and **one
reader — the auditor — caught both.** Mis-credited to a single author it reads as a slip. Correct, it
is evidence that **idiom transplant is a trap the reader catches and the author cannot**: both of us
knew the source ratifiers well, and that familiarity is exactly what made the transplant feel safe.

### Two corrections to my own records, and a fifth sweep variant that eats commit messages

Filed as CONTENT rather than as a commit message, deliberately — that choice is the finding.

**Correction 1 — nothing of `mainC`'s ever rode along in my commit.** I reported on the bus that
`5bdf59f5` swept their uncommitted section and that I had named it in the message. Both halves are
false. They committed it themselves as `a20274c4` at **02:32:24**, fourteen seconds before mine at
**02:32:38**, and `git show 5bdf59f5 -- <file>` contains **zero** of their lines. Corrected to them
on the bus.

**Correction 2 — I twice reported a commit as failed when it had not failed**, and used
`git rev-parse HEAD` as evidence, which handed me `c5a6f030` — another session's commit entirely. In
a clone five agents commit to, **HEAD is not a receipt for your commit.** Verify by content
(`git log -S '<distinctive phrase>'`), never by HEAD.

**The TOCTOU hole in the check I codified hours ago.** The rule was: before committing a shared file,
`git diff -- <file> | grep '^\+(#|##|###)'` and look for another owner's heading. I ran it, saw
`mainC`'s heading, and concluded their work would ride along — **true when taken, false when acted
on**, because they committed in the gap. I then wrote that stale answer into a permanent commit
message as fact. There is no window small enough here; the fix is to verify the **artifact** instead
of the **intention**, after the fact: `git show <sha> -- <file> | grep -E '^\+(#|##|###) '`. A commit
is immutable, so that check has no window at all. Keep the pre-commit grep — it is what makes you
look — but never let its answer into the message as a claim about another person.

**FIFTH VARIANT — `no changes added to commit` is not failure; it is "someone else already committed
your work".** Twice tonight my pathspec commit printed the whole status dump and committed nothing,
and both times the cause was another session committing that file **seconds earlier**, taking my
uncommitted edit with it. `13ff61d6` (auditor, 02:38:26) carries my probe-line removal; the records
above went in under a subject that is not the one I wrote.

This variant is different in kind from the other four and worse in one specific way: **the content
survives and the commit message dies.** The other four misattribute *work*; this one silently discards
the *reasoning* — my analysis of two of my own errors existed only in a message that never landed, and
nothing anywhere would have shown it was missing. The empty-input signature exactly: a check whose
failure mode is indistinguishable from success, because "nothing to commit" is also what a no-op
prints.

**So: durable records go in the FILE, and the commit message only points at them.** A message is the
one artifact in this repo that another session can destroy without leaving a trace.

### HG-3 — closing a rule-box without retiring the rule

The one genuinely-live row out of the C34 disposition, and it needed splitting rather than ticking.
The box reported that `BACKLOG-DISPATCH-QUEUE.md` TOP-40 #6 served `reviewer-escalation-and-human-gate-policy.md:22`
as `none` lane with **no blocker**, while the file's own dependency graph one section down reads
`H4 curves + H5 winners → HG-1 → HG-2/HG-3` with HG-1 open.

It carried **two** claims: a specific defect, and a general rule — *queue classification should consult
the owning handoff's dependency graph, not just the checkbox*. Ticking it whole would have closed the
defect and **retired the rule into silence**, which is precisely the failure `mainC` spent tonight
finding five instances of. So:

**(a) The specific half was already fixed** — TOP-40 #6 now reads `⛔ BLOCKED 2026-07-29` quoting this
box, and HG-1 is still open, so the blocker it names is current rather than stale.

**(b) The general half is now code.** `backlog_row_check.py` gained a fourth screening signal:
`dependency_prereqs()` parses a `## Dependency Graph` block and gives every id every upstream id on
its line; `blocking_dependency_graph()` refuses a row while a prerequisite's own box is open. This is
the one block that neither the row text nor `blocking_children()` can see — the row screens ready
because *nothing in it or under it says otherwise*, which is exactly how the queue served it.

Narrow by construction, per the tool's standing caveat: a real `## Dependency Graph` heading is
required (an arrow in prose is not one), the box's id must match as a **prefix** so a row merely
mentioning `HG-3` is not treated as being it, and an unresolvable prerequisite fails **toward
dispatchable** — refusing real work is this tool's settled costlier error.

**Measured: 12 of 2,177 open boxes (0.55%)**, the same band as the tool's three existing signals, and
every hit reads verbatim as gated — `HG-2/HG-3` on open `HG-1`, `E-3/E-4/E-5` on open `E-2`,
`LB-3/LB-4/LB-6` on open `LB-1`, `RM-7/RM-8` on open `RM-4`. Six tests, 71 passed, and the six were
re-run under `-k` to confirm they are **collected and counted**, not silently deselected.

**The mutation test is the one that matters**: closing the prerequisite must silence the signal. If it
fired with `HG-1` closed too, it would be keyed on the graph alone and would refuse those twelve rows
forever. It goes silent, so it is keyed on prerequisite *state*.

**And my first corpus measurement was vacuous**: it reported `0 open boxes / 0.00%` across 460 files,
because `_boxes()` strips the state — an open box is `""`, not `" "` — so my filter excluded every
row. A 0.00% false-positive rate is a *lovely* number to report and it was pure artifact. What caught
it was not reading the number but asserting the input size (`assert total_open > 500`). Ninth face of
tonight's catalogue, and mine.


### Freeze-window work — the two remaining merge conflicts, characterised read-only

Written during the freeze window and appended on lift.

Probed with `git merge-tree --write-tree main merge/reconcile-0205` — pure computation, no index,
no worktree, no branch touched. That mattered here: this clone still carries a stale cherry-pick in
`.git`, so a test merge in place would have been a bad idea.

**Two conflicting paths, confirming the count — but they are not the same kind of conflict, and the
"both are pure churn" premise is half wrong.**

- `progress/2026-08/2026-08-12.md` — one hunk, genuinely churn. Ours = 121 lines of lane sections,
  theirs = 158 lines of AutoKernel sections, **exactly one overlapping line and it is blank**,
  neither side a subset. Two disjoint append-sets at the same tail. **Union; order irrelevant.**
- `handoffs/active/tool-output-compression.md` — two hunks, each **one line vs one line**. Both sides
  edited the *same* line, which is the opposite of an append. **A union here writes two contradictory
  bullets about the same pin bump.**

**And the pick is settled by fact, not judgment: take theirs.** Main's two lines cite section anchors
`P2.6.1` / `P2.6` in `user-facing-harness-index.md`. That file is 27 lines on both branches and has
**zero** `P2.x` matches of any kind — it was restructured to the thin-row contract (`UFH-01`…) on
2026-08-10. Main's anchors are dangling on both sides; the merge side's anchor-free wording is the
correct post-restructure form.

**The methodology note, because I hit the vacuous read twice in five minutes getting here.** My first
"proof" that the anchors were dangling was `git show <ref>:<path> 2>/dev/null | grep -c` returning 0
on both branches. The file had read **0 lines** — the command errored and my `2>/dev/null` laundered
it into a plausible zero. That is mainA's seventh face, and I walked into it while writing about it.
What rescued it was asserting the *input size* rather than re-reading the count: 0 lines is impossible
for a live handoff. Re-run bare, it is 27 lines and the 0 anchor-matches is a real negative.

**The conclusion survived; the evidence for it did not.** That is the uncomfortable version of this
class — I would have shipped a correct answer resting on a broken proof, and nothing in the output
would have shown it.

### The freeze set was in my own output, and I filtered it out

The coordinator extended the freeze from two paths to three after `mainA` pointed out the method error:
the frozen set had been derived from the paths **observed to conflict**, not the paths that **could**
conflict. I re-derived the right one independently — 22 paths changed on `main` since merge-base
`921113ed`, 67 on the merge branch, intersection exactly three — matching `mainA` and the coordinator.

**My share of that error is the useful part.** My `merge-tree` report an hour earlier said "two
conflicts" and characterised both, which reads as confirmation that two was the number that mattered.
The third path was *in my own probe output*: `git merge-tree` printed
`Auto-merging handoffs/active/intake-derived-work-2026-07-25.md`, and I filtered it out because I was
grepping for `CONFLICT` and treated `Auto-merging` as noise. An agent was still committing to that file.

**And checking it exactly turns the mistake into a cheaper tool than the one that caught it.** The
`Auto-merging` lines from `git merge-tree --write-tree --name-only main merge/reconcile-0205` are
**byte-identical** to the two-diff intersection — `diff -q` clean, all three paths. So one read-only
command yields both sets: `Auto-merging` is the at-risk set to **freeze**, `CONFLICT` is the subset to
**resolve**. It cross-validates the intersection method rather than replacing it, and needs no
merge-base lookup.

**Why the intersection is the correct basis, as mechanism rather than rule.** An `Auto-merging` path is
one *both* sides touched; it merged cleanly today only because the edits happened not to overlap. One
more commit to either side and it conflicts. Observed-conflicts is therefore a subset of at-risk **by
construction** — and precisely the subset that has *already* gone wrong. Freezing it treats a lagging
indicator as a leading one.

**The verification face here is a new one** — not empty input, not a wrong key, not a stale read. The
right datum was present in the output and **my filter discarded it**, because the filter was keyed to
the shape of the answer I expected rather than the question I was asking. When you grep a tool's
output you inherit the tool's vocabulary; read the whole output once before writing the grep.

### The bus wipe had one cause, and it was `git clean -ffdx` in `/workspace`

Drafted under the 09:25Z commit freeze and appended on lift. Held in `/mnt/raid0/llm/tmp/` rather than
`/workspace/tmp/` — applying the finding below, since a scratch dir *inside* the repo is untracked and
would be swept by the very command being diagnosed.

**Cause established, ruled in by scope rather than plausibility.** The sweep took far more than the
bus: `cache/`, `iq2_arch_eval.py`, `q4_architect_verdicts.json`, `opencode.json`,
`artifacts/operator/unblock-receipts/`, `logs/coordinator_daemon.out`, `logs/hub_supervisor.out` — all
gone. Quantified: untracked entries **149 → 3**, total dirty **232 → 56**. A `tmpreaper`-style cleaner
over `logs/` or `/tmp` cannot explain `cache/` and stray repo-root scripts; a targeted delete cannot
explain all of them in one instant.

**The double force is proven, not inferred.** `/workspace/worktrees` is gone — 29 nested repositories.
I had measured hours earlier (`5df3c9eb`) that `git clean -ndx` prints `Would skip repository` for all
29 and removes **none**; only `-ffdx` removes them, as a single `Would remove worktrees/`. The second
`f` was typed. That commit's comment reads *"the only thing standing between `git clean -ffdx` and 29
live worktrees is that nobody types the second `f`"* — written at 08:20Z, falsified within the hour.

**`a70dbe1a` is exonerated as the deleter and load-bearing as the enabler.** The `auditor` is right
that `git rm --cached` cannot delete an untracked file. But its other half added `.gitignore:101`
(`cursors/`) and `:105` (`logs/bus_supervisor.pid`), converting the bus runtime from **tracked — and
therefore immune to `git clean` at any force level** — into ignored, and so `-x`-eligible. Reporting
only the exoneration understates how the two halves combined.

**Damage, after correcting my own alarm.** I was one command from reporting *"inference's 15 live
campaign trees destroyed"*. False: their trees are at `/mnt/raid0/llm/autokernel/worktrees`, outside
`/workspace`, and still resolve. Committed work survives everywhere — deleting a worktree does not
touch the object store — and the branches are intact under **prefixed** names
(`codex/rvp-t0-1-close-20260811`, `codex/sc22-wgm-root-20260811`, …). My first lookup reported
`NO BRANCH` for all six purely because I guessed unprefixed names: a naming assumption inside a damage
assessment, which would have escalated a recoverable state into a catastrophic one. Unrecoverable:
**uncommitted** content in those trees, the bus runtime, and the logs.

**What I could not determine, and I am not inferring it.** Who ran it, and the exact timestamp. No
artifact records the command, because *the clean removed the logs that would have carried it* —
self-concealing in the same shape as face 14. What would settle it: shell history or a terminal
transcript from whichever session was doing worktree-isolation work between 08:05Z (I counted 29
worktrees) and 08:25Z (wipe reported). `a70dbe1a`'s author is the obvious person to **ask**; proximity
is not evidence and I am not naming a suspect from it.

**The structural lesson, and it is the second instance in twelve hours.** C45 (the audit log) and
`a70dbe1a` (the bus) both converted *merge safety* into *clean exposure*. `mainC` named the write-side
half: snapshot **before** untracking, because git-recovery recovers only what git already had — which
by definition excludes everything the untracking is about. This is the read-side half:

> **The moment a path becomes ignored it becomes `clean -x` fodder.** Untracking live runtime should
> move it *out* of the repo, not merely hide it *from* git.

The worktree-isolation design already does exactly that for the five mains
(`/mnt/raid0/llm/worktrees/mains`), which is why that placement is right and why the 29 trees inside
the repo were the ones that died.

### C50 — the probe caught a defect in its own fix

`1fae78dc` made `_eligible` dereference `spec_ref`. The assignment was the **probe** that proves it
works and would catch a regression, and building it was worth more than the fix was.

**The cause, stated once:** `session_bus_coordinator.py:1633` writes `"status": "READY"` as a literal
and copies `spec_ref` beside it. `_eligible` then checked status, deps, gates, lane and load — all
from the queue row — and never opened `spec_ref`. Carried into messages at three call sites,
dereferenced at none. Rot was **undetectable by construction**; a row closed on 2026-07-29 stayed
READY for fourteen days because nothing could see otherwise.

**The probe found the fix inert.** My first C50 tests called `spec_ref_state()` directly — that proves
the *helper*, not the thing that decides dispatch. Written against the consumer instead, the probe
dispatched a stale row on its first run **while every helper test stayed green**. Cause:
`spec_ref_state(spec_ref, repo_root=REPO_ROOT)` binds the default **at import**, so the consumer
always resolved against the real repo and no caller could vary it. The isolated tests passed a root
explicitly and therefore never touched the default — the one path production uses. Now late-bound.

> **A default argument evaluated at import is a constant wearing a parameter's clothes.** The tests
> that pass it explicitly are the ones that cannot see the bug.

Mutation-verified in both directions, because one direction only proves the test runs: remove the
dereference → 2 failed; revert the late binding → 2 failed; source restored byte-identical. 310 pass.

**Split the task box rather than ticking it.** The dereference *detects* rot; it does not remove the
dependency on line numbers, which was the other half of the filed item. Six of seven anchors point at
a tree-diagram branch, a `###` heading or a prose bullet, and the task_id's halves disagree —
`--013-` vs `L534`, where box #13 is at line 59. Re-anchoring on row identity stays open and is
written that way.


### C49 — declared, not inferred, and the consumer check found what the unit tests could not

`_is_operator_item` and `action_required` were near-disjoint: 267 live inbox rows, **116** carrying
`action_required`, **8** satisfying the predicate, **7** overlapping. Misclassified in both directions.
The detail that settles the design: **all 8 were `kind: defect`, and not one was a `token-request`** —
the only kind that means a human must sign. The predicate was dominated by fleet-internal engineering.

Now: `payload.operator_signature_needed: true`, plus `token-request`, which stays because a token *is*
the operator's signature — dropping it would fix the false positives by making the channel silent.
Dropped `defect` and `CRITICAL`: urgency is not the-human-must-decide. Live re-measure **8 → 0**.

**The migration cost, said plainly:** nothing written before today declares the marker, so a genuine
non-token operator need goes unescalated until its author declares it. One row in this corpus.

**Two things the consumer check caught that unit tests could not.** Narrowing broke `test_c20_bypass…`,
whose fixture leaned on `kind: defect` qualifying — fixed at the *fixture*, not by widening the
predicate back, because the test is about bypass idempotence and not about classification. Then my
first fixture attempt defaulted the declaration inside the shared `_op_msg` helper and broke
`test_c27c_…_stay_quiet`: **a fixture change that deleted the very distinction another test existed to
check.** Declaration is per-caller now.

And one observation I am not fixing because it is not mine: the C20 assertion reads
`f"{coordinator._OPERATOR_ESCALATION_MARKER} m-42"` — it pins the constant *by reference*, so mutating
that constant moves both sides and the test still passes. A guard that cannot detect a change to the
thing it names is face 4 wearing a variable.

### E8-PANELS-c — the surfaces existed; the guard protecting them was vacuous

Screened `autopilot-dashboard-fidelity-audit-2026-07-22.md:343` by `--ref`, claimed it, and found
both requested deliverables already in place: absolute `all_tasks_done` at `handoffs.html:620`, and
the newly-filed series at `:943`/`:994-995`. The percentage was *kept* rather than removed — a later
design decision, commented in place — but scope-labelled ("% done · open scope" beside "% done · all
scope"), which answers the row's complaint by disambiguation instead of deletion.

**The real defect was the frontend-contract test.** It asserted
`assertNotIn("const pctAll = bk.pct_all_done", html)` — and that exact expression exists nowhere in
the file. So the guard passed while `bk.pct_all_done` was still being rendered through a *different*
expression at `:704-705`. **A guard that forbids one way of writing a thing does not forbid the
thing.** It would not have noticed an unlabelled percentage returning, which is precisely the failure
the row was filed about.

Rewritten to assert the property: if `bk.pct_all_done` is rendered at all, it must carry its scope
label and appear beside the open-scope figure.

**Mutation-checked, because a guard I cannot make fail is the defect I was removing.** Stripping the
two scope labels while leaving the render in place makes the new assertion fail
(`'% done · all scope' not found`); the *old* assertion passed on that same mutation. Restored,
42/42 green.

This is the fourth face of tonight's verification class and the first that is purely a *test* defect:
input silently empty, key measuring the wrong property, target read from the wrong plane — and now
**assertion pinned to a spelling rather than a property**. All four pass for the wrong reason; none
of the four tests catches the others.


### `mainB` — my batch-8 section was filed under `mainA`'s heading

`mainA` found that every section they wrote overnight sat as a `###` nested beneath `mainD`'s single
`##` lane header — *"not lost, not misdated, MISATTRIBUTED"*. I checked my own and had the same
defect in miniature: my `##` lane header was correct, but the batch-8 block I appended afterwards
landed at end-of-file, which by then was **below `mainA`'s header**. Six hours of `cat >>` and the
container was wrong the whole time while every individual append looked right.

Moved my block — and only mine — up under my own heading. The move is 38 insertions / 37 deletions,
i.e. the same content relocated. **The guard that made this safe fired on the first attempt:** I
asserted the tail was exclusively mine before moving it, and it was not — `mainC` had appended after
me — so the naive version would have dragged their section along. Re-bounded to stop at the next
heading of any level. `mainC`'s block is still under `mainA`'s header; that is theirs to move, not
mine.

Adopted `mainA`'s pre-commit check for this file: `git diff -- progress/2026-08/2026-08-12.md` and
confirm every hunk is yours. Ran it here; every added line traces to my own paragraphs. Also changing
my own habit — **append under my heading, never at end-of-file**, because end-of-file is a moving
target in a file five agents share.

The class is the one running through tonight: *absence read as membership.* "My sections are in the
file" is a different check from "my sections are under my heading", and only the second is what the
operator reads.

### `mainB` — batch 8: the circular-import row, and a fail-open that outlived its reason

`mainC` swept the stale claims and freed `model-stack-update-pipeline-audit.md:637` — a row I had
declined an hour earlier *solely* because a claim from a session that died 14 days ago sat on it.
Screened, claimed, worked. Also released my own stale claim (318h): its row no longer exists anywhere
in `handoffs/`, so releasing rather than re-affirming was the honest call.

**The row's stated cause is GONE; the fail-open it justified is not.** The row asks to untangle the
`scripts.server` import cycle "so the fail-open then stops masking filter behavior". Re-derived: the
cycle **no longer reproduces** — 3 bare imports and 4 package-order imports of
`runtime_facts_manifest` / `stack_paths` / `stack_manifest` / `orchestrator_stack` all import
cleanly. Something between 2026-07-24 and now fixed it (the `scripts/server/` → `src/config/` move is
the likely candidate).

But `expected_stack_services()` still carried both degraded paths at **`logger.debug`**, and that is
what made them masks rather than fallbacks:

- filter failure → returns the **unfiltered** manifest, so ports belonging to other NUMA modes render
  as "expected" and look exactly like a correct list;
- manifest unavailable → returns `[]`, which renders as a **healthy empty stack** rather than a read
  failure.

Both now log at warning, naming what was substituted and why. **The fail-open itself is kept
deliberately** — for a dashboard panel, over-reporting beats dropping the panel — because the defect
was never the fallback, it was the silence. With the cycle gone, anything the handler catches now is
a real filter defect, which is precisely the case that must not be quiet.

Six tests, including two that matter beyond the happy path: a **negative control** asserting the
healthy path does *not* warn (an alarm that always fires is not an alarm), and a parametrised test
pinning that the original cycle stays fixed — so a regression re-files the row instead of silently
restoring the justification for the mask. 158 passed alongside; the 3 failures in
`test_dashboard_helpers` are the known pre-existing P0-0 priors set.

**Row left OPEN**, not flipped: its literal ask is "untangle the import cycle", and I did not untangle
it — I found it already untangled and fixed the residue it left behind. Annotated with both halves so
the owner can close it on the evidence rather than on my say-so.

### `mainB` — the verification class closed at six signs, from five agents

`mainC` asked me to word their instance into the entry since I hold it; `mainA` contributed the sixth
in the same hour. Both are in `feedback_vacuous_verification_empty_input`, credited.

**Fifth (`mainC`)** — a tripwire mutation-checked with an **untracked** probe file. `git grep` only
sees *tracked* files, so the probe was invisible, the check passed, and the tripwire looked inert. The
mutation was real; the *instrument* could not see it. This generalises the remedy rather than
replacing it: mutation-test the guard, **and confirm the mutation is visible to the tool doing the
looking.** A probe the instrument cannot observe is the same as no probe.

**Sixth (`mainA`)** — containers rather than content. *"Is my work in the file"* and *"is my work
under MY heading"* are different questions needing different tools: the first is a grep and it
passes; the second means walking **up** to the nearest `##` and reading whose name is on it. Their
root-cause framing is the part worth keeping — **`cat >> file` shows you nothing about what precedes
your text**, so appending is the one edit where the structure you are joining is invisible from the
edit you are making. Not three lapses; a tool shape that hides the only thing that matters.

The entry now carries six signs from five agents in one night: empty input, key wider than the
property, target read from the working tree, assertion pinned to a spelling, probe outside the tool's
universe, container unchecked. **None of the six tests catches the others**, which is the argument for
keeping them in one place rather than as six war stories.

**The P0 merge is staged**, my seven paths only, verified at content level with all three stages
captured before any `git add` — the discipline this class exists to produce, applied under time
pressure rather than at leisure. Twelve paths remain unmerged and none is mine; both progress files
are among them and want a union plus re-derivation against current main.

### `mainB` — the shared-file class, as ONE note instead of four disclosures

`coordinator-agent` asked for this consolidated rather than scattered. Written up as
`feedback_shared_file_whole_file_operations` in fleet memory.

**A file several agents append to concurrently punishes every whole-file operation.** Four mains hit
it in one night on the two progress files — but in **two distinct sub-shapes**, and saying so is
worth more than a symmetric count:

- **SWEEP** — a pathspec commit is *file*-limited, not *hunk*-limited: `git commit -- shared.md`
  captures every uncommitted hunk in that file, including another agent's mid-edit. `--amend` is
  worse; it re-reads the whole index. `mainD`'s `ece707b6` absorbed `mainA`'s entire lane section,
  and the commit *fixing* that misattribution was itself swept the same way. Check:
  `git diff -- <file>` immediately before committing.
- **RE-PARENT** — `cat >>` writes at end-of-file, and end-of-file is a **moving target**. Your `###`
  becomes a child of whichever `##` was written last. `mainC` and I both hit this; content present
  and correct, only the *container* wrong, so every content check passes. Check:
  `grep -n "^## " <file> | tail -1`.

**I checked which one was mine rather than assuming symmetry.** All six of my progress commits touched
exactly one file, and I ran the hunk check on the recent ones — so I did **not** sweep anyone. My
instance is the re-parent variant. Claiming a sweep I had not verified would be the misattributed
self-disclosure `mainD` warned about earlier tonight, one level up.

**Why it fooled experienced agents repeatedly:** appending is the one edit where *the structure you
are joining is invisible from the edit you are making*. `cat >>` shows nothing about what precedes
your text; a pathspec shows nothing about who else is mid-edit in that path. Both are silent and both
look exactly like success. Pathspec discipline solves **cross-file** contamination and does nothing
for **within-file** contamination — which is the gap the fleet had not named.

**Not fixed by rewriting the shared file**, per the operator's ruling, which was right. Move only your
own contiguous block, bound it at the next heading of **any** level — a naive to-end-of-file move
drags the next agent's section along, caught in practice on my first attempt — assert the block is
exclusively yours before moving, and run the diff check after.

**Noted and not acted on:** the C39 signer is PARKED until the operator wakes, with two known defects
including `mainA`'s point that a hard refuse-on-index-exists would have **broken this host** —
`mint_receipt` here is verify-or-continue and re-run-after-crash is the designed recovery path.
Nobody edits a signer tonight; I have not been near it.

### `mainB` — the catalogue reaches eight, and a near-miss of my own in the writing of it

`mainC` routed a seventh face explicitly to me; `mainA`'s eighth had been pending since 02:28. Both
are now in `feedback_vacuous_verification_empty_input`, credited.

**Seventh (`mainC`)** — a trust-boundary guard's only test asserted inside `main()` behind
`if __name__ == "__main__"`, in a file named `test_*.py` in a `tests/` directory. Run directly it
passes all 20 checks and is mutation-verified load-bearing. **Under pytest it collects zero tests.**
Genuinely distinct from the six: the check is correct, complete *and* passing — it simply contributes
nothing to the suite anyone runs, while wearing that runner's naming convention, which is what makes
it invisible. The question it adds: *does the tool that reports pass/fail actually COUNT this check?*

**Eighth (`mainA`)** — `cmd 2>/dev/null | wc -l` launders a *failed* command into `0`. They sized four
conflicts, got all zeros, and caught it only because all-zeros is the vacuous signature; the cause was
`git show :2:` failing on already-resolved paths. `git ls-files -u` is the non-lying form.

**And I nearly shipped the exact defect while filing them.** My first edit updated the MEMORY.md index
line to say "eight faces" — and the entry edit **failed on a stale anchor**, so for a moment the index
advertised content the entry did not contain. Same shape as `aa03f0e0` earlier tonight, where a commit
message described a note its edit had not applied. Caught on the assertion error rather than
discovered later, and fixed before committing: the entry now carries both, verified by grep, and the
index matches.

That is the third time tonight the failure has been *my own claim outrunning my own edit*. The
pattern is narrower than the catalogue: **when an edit and its announcement are written in the same
breath, the announcement lands even if the edit does not.** Assert the edit applied before writing the
claim — which is precisely what the `assert anchor in s` did here.

### `mainB` — the verification catalogue, finished as one artifact

`coordinator-agent` asked for this consolidated rather than left scattered, and they were right that
it is the most reusable thing this lane produced tonight. It now exists as a citable fleet document:
**`docs/guides/agent-workflows/verification-failure-catalogue.md`**, indexed in that folder's
`INDEX.md`.

**Eight faces, five agents, one night, all measured rather than theorised** — every entry is an
instance that actually occurred, with its mechanism, its tell, its own test, and credit to whoever
found it. The organising claim is the one worth defending: *a failing check is cheap; a check that
passes for the wrong reason is expensive, because it also supplies confidence.*

The remedy is stated once at the top — **mutation-test the guard; if you cannot make it fail, it is
not a guard** — with the two follow-ups that three of the eight faces survive: is the mutation
**visible** to the tool doing the looking (face 5), and is the check **counted** by the tool that
reports pass/fail (face 7).

Why it needed to be one artifact rather than eight findings: **none of the eight tests catches the
others.** Face 1's test passes cleanly on a face-2 defect — mainA demonstrated that against their own
check. Scattered, each face reads as one agent's war story; together they are a minute-long checklist
keyed on the diff, which is the form that actually fires. Every agent involved already knew "verify
your work", and knowing it prevented none of these.

Two meta-observations are carried in the artifact because they cost real time tonight: a retraction
that **publishes its mechanism** is worth more than one that merely withdraws (one refutation here
was wrong, and the retraction corrected a mechanism already written down under the wrong
description); and **when an edit and its announcement are written in the same breath, the announcement
lands even if the edit does not** — two commit messages tonight described notes their edits had
failed to apply, both mine.

Provenance is recorded per face rather than collectively, because three agents corrected
misattributions tonight and a catalogue that blurred credit would undercut its own subject.


### `mainB` — merge paths verified inside the commit; progress write deferred under the freeze

Written 02:5xZ, **held in scratch** under `coordinator-agent`'s two-path freeze and appended after the
lift. Deferring the write, not the wrap-up.

**My seven merge paths verified inside commit `66f40eeb` itself**, not in the index and not in a
working tree: `git show 66f40eeb:<path>` for each. All seven present, zero conflict markers, line
counts identical to my resolution (2295 / 1039 / 1145 / 1918 / 1 / 604 / 154). Then the content check
that matters — for each of the six markdown paths, every substantive line either side ADDED relative
to the merge base is present in the committed blob: **zero lost from either lane**. Line counts alone
would not catch a clobber that preserved length, which is why the added-lines test is the one I ran.

That is the third verification of these paths, each against a different plane — working tree, index,
and now commit — because tonight produced eight distinct ways a check can pass for the wrong reason
and reading the wrong plane was face 3.

**Freeze compliance, checked rather than assumed.** Both frozen paths were already clean in my tree
before the instruction arrived: `git status --short -- progress/2026-08/2026-08-12.md
handoffs/active/tool-output-compression.md` returns empty, so I was holding no uncommitted edit that
could re-conflict the merge or be swept by someone else's commit. I have never touched
`tool-output-compression.md` at all.

**Why the freeze is the right shape.** The merge is adjudicated safe and committed; it cannot land
while those two files move, because every commit to them re-conflicts it. The chain is short and
worth stating plainly: no merge → no push → the fleet is not committed-and-pushed → no OP-16 relay →
`inference` gets no reboot and no CPU IQK campaign. Two files stand between here and a reboot, and the
cheapest possible contribution is to stop typing into them.
